Taylor Sheridan’s ‘Yellowstone’ Spin-Off Officially Returns After Surprise Break

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

When it was reported that Yellowstone was ending, no one knew what the future would look like for what was once the biggest show on television. There were internal wrangles, and after five years, it seemed like some cast and crew members were ready for something different. So it came as a bit of a surprise when CBS and Paramount announced a new spin-off, but it wouldn't be like anything viewers had seen before. The new series would take a procedural approach and would follow one of the least explored characters in the flagship series, Kayce Dutton (Luke Grimes).

The problem was that Grimes wasn't interested in playing Kayce after Yellowstone, and he had been vocal about it. "I didn’t think I wanted to come back to it, because I felt like Kayce’s story ended so perfectly in the first show," the actor admitted in an interview with THR. "And I was like, well, what are we gonna do now? He’s got his dream life. Everything he’s been fighting for, he’s gotten. He literally rode off into the sunset. And what sort of breaks that world open for him is that that dream life became no longer possible." With a bit of convincing from showrunner Spencer Hudnut and support from Taylor Sheridan, Grimes put the cowboy hat back on, and Marshals arrived earlier this year, becoming a ratings juggernaut for CBS.

Season 2 airs this fall, but the network announced the show would take a two-week break, meaning no new episode on Sunday, November 8. However, Marshals is back on schedule this Sunday, November 15, with an episode that could become a turning point for some character dynamics. In Marshals Season 2, Episode 5, "Roughstock," Cal (Logan Marshall-Green) goes to Salt Lake to see a cancer specialist, and Belle (Arielle Kebbel) tags along without anyone else knowing. Is this the beginning of a new romance, and what effect will it have on the team? Meanwhile, Andrea (Ash Santos) temporarily takes the reins in Cal's absence, and problems arise when Kayce steps outside the marshal's jurisdiction to help his friend Mo (Mo Brings Plenty) after Mo's nephew goes missing.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

After this break, the show will air uninterrupted until around the holiday period. On November 22, the team is back together, and this time Kayce and Belle go undercover. There will be some guest appearances from Beau Knapp, Judd Lormand, Marque Richardson, and Carter Faith. On November 29, the team hunts down an international fugitive. Meanwhile, Kayce starts a new project while his relationship with Tate (Brecken Merrill) remains strained. Scarlett Burke guest stars.

Watch new episodes of Marshals on Sundays on CBS and stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

Fantastic News for CoreWeave Stock Investors

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Wall Street may have been wrong about the company after all.

Despite starting the year on a strong note, shares of CoreWeave (CRWV -10.63%) are down by nearly 13% year-to-date, as of writing. What's going on with the company? On the one hand, CoreWeave is cashing in on massive demand for artificial intelligence (AI) compute. However, there are several issues with the company, including the massive debt it is now carrying on its balance sheet. That said, CoreWeave recently reported its third-quarter results, and they were full of bullish signs for the company. Let's dig in.

The market had high expectations for CoreWeave ahead of its third-quarter earnings, and the neocloud company blew them out of the water. Revenue increased by 168% year over year to $3.7 billion. The top line grew much faster than operating expenses, resulting in strong growth in operating income.

CoreWeave's operating income for the period was $207 million, substantially better than the $52 million recorded in the year-ago period. Its operating margin also increased to 6%, up from the 4% reported in the prior-year quarter. CoreWeave remains unprofitable, and the net loss per share widened to $1.27 in the period, compared to $0.22 in the prior-year quarter.

However, the fact that CoreWeave's revenue growth accelerated for the first time in a while suggests there is still strong demand for the company's services, which helps justify the massive spending contributing to the worsening bottom-line numbers. We can also look at CoreWeave's revenue backlog, which was $132.5 billion for the period, up 138% year over year.

CoreWeave also raised its guidance for the fiscal year 2026. It now expects revenue between $13 billion and $13.3 billion, up from its previous range of $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion. The company is now also projecting it will finish the year with a revenue run rate of $20 billion to $21 billion; CoreWeave's trailing-12-month revenue is $9.89 billion. It's not surprising that the stock soared on the heels of another excellent quarterly performance.

Can CoreWeave maintain its momentum? The company faces at least three major risks. First, during the recent U.S. midterm elections, voters overwhelmingly elected officials who ran on pumping the brakes on data center expansion. If regulatory uncertainty in the AI industry increases over the next few years, it could weigh on CoreWeave's financial results.

Second, the company's significant debt and net losses remain an issue. Third, there is customer concentration. According to some reports, Microsoft (MSFT +0.27%) could opt to work with fewer neocloud partners. This single customer accounted for about 67% of CoreWeave's revenue last year, so if Microsoft cuts ties with CoreWeave, it would be catastrophic for the company.

Can CoreWeave perform well despite these problems? In my view, it could, although the stock remains somewhat risky. Consider the first risk. CoreWeave's management does not expect a significant impact from increased regulatory scrutiny on the business. And the company has also been building capacity outside the U.S., which helps mitigate its regulatory exposure in the country.

Second, CoreWeave's significant debt is necessary for the company to continue meeting demand for its services. In fact, CoreWeave is largely sold out through 2027, according to management, while there is already strong demand for 2028. What about customer concentration? If Microsoft decides to work with fewer neoclouds, it could be very bad for CoreWeave, or it could be very good.

CoreWeave is one of the largest neoclouds, and if it remains in Microsoft's smaller list of partners as the tech giant continues to demand more services, that would mean a higher budget distributed among fewer providers, potentially leading to higher revenue for CoreWeave. Customer concentration remains a meaningful risk for investors to monitor, but for now, CoreWeave appears likely to continue performing well despite it. And even after its post-earnings jump, the stock looks attractive, particularly for investors comfortable with volatility.

The unfolding Jensen Huang scandal: a timeline

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Nvidia co-founder and (now former) CEO Jensen Huang has never been as flashy or controversy-prone as AI leaders like OpenAI’s Sam Altman or SpaceXAI’s Elon Musk. Now, the 63-year-old industry kingpin has admitted that he cheated on his wife of 40 years in affairs that could have ramifications beyond Huang’s personal life and U.S. national security.

U.S. officials believe that Huang’s likely romantic interest, Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, is a Chinese spy who has been feeding information back to Beijing. (Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen, is of no relation to Jensen Huang.)

Megaspeed is a Singapore-based AI infrastructure company that reportedly purchased $4.8 billion in Nvidia chips over the last two years. The U.S. and Singapore have both previously investigated Megaspeed over suspicions that the firm was illegally sharing chips with China. The Justice Department has since taken over the federal investigation into the company, The New York Times has reported.

The story took a sudden new turn on Wednesday. Citing five people who worked at Nvidia, the Times reported that Huang also had an extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, the company’s vice president of health care, who reports directly to him. Nvidia, Huang and Powell have not confirmed the report. Reached by a Times reporter, Huang reportedly said, “I don’t think you should be talking to me directly,” before hanging up.

Separately, the Wall Street Journal reported that Nvidia’s board is preparing to oust Huang, and Nvidia’s shares fell in after-hours trading.

The U.S. and China are embroiled in a fierce technological arms race to build the most advanced AI infrastructure, which could shape each country’s economic future, military prowess, and capacity for innovation. Nvidia is valuable in this competition as one of the world’s largest makers of AI chips, which are essential for accelerating AI development. But as an American company, Nvidia faces government restrictions on the sale of its most powerful chips to Chinese entities.

As the controversy unfolds, we are organizing the most relevant events into a reverse-chronological timeline to keep you up to date.

Nvidia confirmed in an SEC filing on Thursday that the board accepted Huang’s resignation on Wednesday. The company’s current CFO, Collete Kress, will take over as interim CEO while still fulfilling her CFO duties. Nvidia is expected to conduct a larger search to determine a permanent successor to Huang.

Harvey C. Jones, a managing partner of Square Wave Ventures and former CEO of Synopsys, was appointed as the company’s first-ever chair of the board of directors. Jones has been on Nvidia’s board since 1993.

After reports of a second extramarital affair, Nvidia’s board reportedly plans to meet to discuss Huang’s future at the company, per the Wall Street Journal. Stock fell about 8% in after hours trading on Wednesday and is down 11% in the past five days.

Huang famously delegates very little and has about 60 direct reports, he has said. The company has not had an obvious successor or a succession plan.

The New York Times reported that Jensen Huang also had an affair with Nvidia vice president of healthcare Kimberly Powell, who reports directly to the CEO.

According to the report, Huang and Powell were captured on film “being affectionate” at a hotel during a conference earlier this year.

Huang has not responded to a request from three Republican congressman to appear for a closed-door briefing for questioning about his affair with an alleged Chinese spy. House China committee Chairman Moolenaar said Wednesday on Fox Business that if Huang doesn’t agree to the briefing, he’ll likely be subpoenaed.

The Nvidia CEO pulled out of upcoming speaking engagements at the ElevenLabs Summit and Anthropic CEO Forum.

Alice Huang and her family reportedly built a network of companies in China and Singapore to acquire Nvidia chips, including founding a Chinese company called Shanghai Century Litong. Founded in 2023, it became an Nvidia-preferred cloud partner, giving it priority access to chips, The Information reported. The Information also reported that Shanghai Century Litong facilitated buying $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia’s chips, including banned chips.

Nvidia’s head lawyer, Tim Teter, intervened in a 2025 U.S. government probe surrounding the suspected smuggling of AI chips by Megaspeed International, Bloomberg reported. Teter contacted Commerce Under Secretary Jeffrey Kessler asking questions about whether the Trump administration played a role in detaining Alice Huang.

In early 2025, Alice Huang and a business partner sought China-based investors for an Asian data center, promising they had no problem in securing GPUs, the Financial Times reported.

On Sunday night, President Trump weighed in on Huang’s controversy and showed support for the Nvidia CEO.

“I have not spoken to him about it. I just know he’s a fine person, but you never know what’s going on,” Trump said.

When asked if Huang should remain chief of Nvidia, Trump said, “I think so. Oh yeah, sure.”

Jensen Huang wrote in a letter to Nvidia employees — which was also published as a press release — that he cheated on his wife.

“I owe my family and all of you an apology,” Huang told his company. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

Huang did not mention Alice Huang by name in the message, but linked his infidelity to “the national security concerns raised in news reports.”

“Nvidia’s Board of Directors has initiated an independent investigation. I support the investigation and will cooperate fully. I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America’s national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American,” Huang wrote.

Nvidia shares slid over 5% the following day.

Three Republican congressmen — Chairman John Moolenaar of the House Select Committee on China, Chairman Brian Mast of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, and Chairman Rick Crawford of the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence — sent a request to Huang for a closed-door joint briefing with their three committees.

“Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the [People’s Republic of China]’s access to sensitive U.S. technology and information,” the chairmen wrote. “The issue of PRC infiltration and access to cutting-edge U.S. technology is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress. It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk.”

U.S. officials had been investigating Alice Huang and Megaspeed for more than a year when the New York Times broke the story in late October that the two executives also had a close relationship.

An Nvidia spokesperson denied that this relationship was something out of the ordinary.

“Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year,” Nvidia spokesperson John Rizzo said. He added that “if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know.”

U.S. officials circulated a secret memo among policymakers that warned that Megaspeed’s Alice Huang, who had a relationship with Jensen Huang, was a spy for China’s Ministry of State Security. This memo was not public knowledge until it was later reported by the New York Times.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio supervised the drafting of the memo.

Around the same time, per the New York Times’ report, U.S. President Donald Trump was briefed about intelligence findings that possible Chinese spy Alice Huang had formed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. Trump did not act on this information.

The Nvidia CEO, an ally of Trump, has repeatedly advocated for the U.S. government to let Nvidia expand its business in China.

Megaspeed was confirmed to be under investigation by both the U.S. and Singaporean governments in October 2025. As a customer of Nvidia, Megaspeed was suspected of helping Chinese companies get around American embargoes on chip exports.

Megaspeed, a neocloud that used Nvidia chips, had been renting computing power to Chinese internet giant Alibaba and other Chinese companies, the Times reported. A Commerce Department official who had visited Megaspeed’s data center in Malaysia in 2024 had found a hoard of Nvidia equipment that was still packed in boxes and the concern was that this equipment was being directly shipped somewhere else, like China, rather than installed and sold as a cloud service.

Jensen Huang had been spotted socializing with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang as early as 2024 in Taiwan, where the Nvidia CEO was born.

These investigations into Megaspeed highlighted concerns about the degree to which Nvidia is able to track its chip exports. At the time, an Nvidia spokesperson told press that the company did not have “any reason to believe products had been diverted.”

“NVIDIA visited multiple Megaspeed sites yet again earlier this week and confirmed what we previously observed — Megaspeed is running a small commercial cloud, like many other companies throughout the world, as allowed by U.S. export control rules,” the Nvidia spokesperson said at the time.

What Trump cases might soon return to the Supreme Court?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

During the 2025-26 term, the Supreme Court handed President Donald Trump defeats on two of his signature initiatives – his efforts to limit birthright citizenship and impose sweeping tariffs – as well as his initial attempt to fire Lisa Cook, a member of the Federal Reserve’s Board of Governors. But those rulings do not necessarily mean the justices have seen the last of any of those issues. In the wake of the court’s decisions in each of the three cases, Trump continues to fight on, which has led (or could lead) to litigation that may reach the court again.

The Democratic Party’s decisive victory in the midterm elections, which gave it a majority in both the U.S. House of Representatives and the Senate, could theoretically hinder Trump’s plans. However, Democrats would need not just a majority to pass legislation curbing the president’s authority but a veto-proof majority: 67 votes in the Senate and 290 in the House, which could be more difficult to obtain.

In late June, a closely divided court rebuffed – at least for the time being – Trump’s effort to fire Cook. By a vote of 5-4, the court permitted Cook to stay at the Fed while she challenges Trump’s attempt to dismiss her.

The dispute began in August 2025, when Trump posted screenshots on social media of a letter to Cook in which he fired her. Trump cited allegations that Cook, who began serving on the Fed in 2022 and whom then-President Joe Biden nominated to a full 14-year term one year later, had committed mortgage fraud in 2021 by listing two different homes – a condo in Georgia and a house in Michigan – as her primary residence on mortgage applications, making her eligible for more favorable terms.

In the wake of Trump’s attempt to dismiss her, Cook went to federal court in Washington, D.C. The lower courts there permitted her to remain in office while her challenge moved forward. The Trump administration then asked the Supreme Court to step in and pause those decisions, but it instead opted to hear oral argument.

In his opinion for the majority, Chief Justice John Roberts explained that (among other things), Trump acted improperly when he fired Cook without notifying her that he intended to do so and without giving her an opportunity to respond to the allegations against her.

Just over five weeks later, White House Deputy Chief of Staff Dan Scavino sent Cook a letter in which he notified her that Trump was “considering” whether to remove her from the Board of Governors and gave her 21 days to respond to the mortgage fraud allegations.

In a five-page letter on Aug. 26, Cook’s attorney, Abbe Lowell, characterized the allegations against her as “unfounded and untrue.” Therefore, he said, any attempt to dismiss Cook based on those accusations would be “corrosive of the independence that Congress sought to preserve” for the Federal Reserve Bank.

On Oct. 7, Trump announced the formation of a three-person committee, made up of senior administration officials, to investigate the mortgage-fraud allegations. He also directed the committee to hold a hearing at the White House on Nov. 5, but the hearing has been rescheduled for Dec. 14 because Cook could not attend on the original date.

Trump indicated that the committee should “submit written findings of fact and conclusions of law to” him and make a recommendation regarding whether Cook should be removed “[a]s soon as practicable after” that. If the committee advises Trump that Cook should be fired, it will almost certainly lead to additional litigation, which likely would return to the Supreme Court no matter how the lower courts rule. The court may then have to decide whether, in accordance with its prior opinion, the process that the Trump administration followed before firing Cook was adequate.

After the Supreme Court issued its decision in June, Cook’s original case returned to the district court for further proceedings. However, on Nov. 6, Cook and Trump asked the district court to temporarily suspend its proceedings in the case. Lowell told the justices that his client and the Trump administration “are discussing a process by which the allegations will be considered by the ‘committee of inquiry,’ including the specifics and deadlines.”

Congress could take a variety of steps to affect Trump’s efforts to fire Cook or future Fed governors: it could, for example, amend the law governing the Fed to define what constitutes “cause” allowing a president to remove a governor, or to outline the exact procedural steps that the president must follow when attempting to fire a governor. However, any such legislation would be (as noted above) potentially subject to a Trump veto. If Trump does fire Cook, the Senate could also refuse to confirm a successor.

On the last day before its summer recess, the Supreme Court struck down Trump’s executive order seeking to end birthright citizenship – the guarantee of citizenship to virtually everyone born in the United States. In Trump v. Barbara, a majority of the court agreed with the challengers that Trump’s order cannot be reconciled with the 14th Amendment to the Constitution, which confers citizenship on anyone “born … in the United States, and subject to the jurisdiction thereof.” Roberts emphasized that the “children born of parents unlawfully or temporarily present in the United States” “satisfy both elements of the Citizenship Clause.” “Under the Constitution,” he concluded, “they are citizens at birth.”

Just over one month later, Trump signed a new executive order that sought to impose different restrictions on birthright citizenship. While his first order had barred children whose parents were either undocumented immigrants or only in the United States temporarily from gaining automatic U.S. citizenship, his Aug. 6 order would deny automatic citizenship to the children of a variety of foreign citizens in the U.S., such as employees at a foreign embassy and employees of international organizations like the United Nations and the World Bank. The order also carved out exemptions from the birthright citizenship rule for any child whose parent was designated as an “alien enemy” under several federal laws; for the children of parents who “engaged in a commercial transaction to purchase or access birthright citizenship for the person, or engaged in fraudulent activity to obtain citizenship”; and where “the person is born in a territory or territorial waters of the United States where citizenship is not conferred by Federal statute.”

On Sept. 4, the Department of Homeland Security issued an interim final rule – that is, a rule issued without first publishing a proposed rule and soliciting comments – to implement the Aug. 6 executive order.

Challenges to the second executive order have arisen in several different courts around the United States. In Barbara, the case in which the justices had struck down Trump’s first executive order, the challengers sought to add plaintiffs and claims to their case to address the second executive order. They argued that both the first and second executive orders “represent efforts to narrow the well-settled meaning of the Citizenship Clause’s language, namely ‘subject to the jurisdiction thereof,’ in order to strip U.S.-born children of citizenship beyond the exceptions set out in” in the Supreme Court’s cases, including Barbara. The challengers also asked U.S. District Judge Joseph Laplante for an injunction that would temporarily bar the Trump administration from enforcing the second executive order against the class of people who would be affected by it.

In an order on Sept. 10, Laplante expressed concerns about the use of the Barbara case to challenge the new executive order. He suggested that the challenge should instead come in a separate new proceeding. On Sept. 23, the challengers withdrew their motions to add claims and plaintiffs and seek preliminary relief from the second executive order.

Trump’s second executive order has also been challenged in the case of CASA v. Trump. Like Barbara, CASA had initially been filed in response to Trump’s first executive order on birthright citizenship. In 2025 CASA went to the Supreme Court, where the justices ruled that federal courts generally lack the authority to issue “universal” or “nationwide” injunctions – orders that bar the federal government from enforcing a policy or law anywhere in the country.

In August 2026, the plaintiffs in CASA asked U.S. District Judge Deborah Boardman to enter a preliminary injunction barring the enforcement of the second executive order. She granted that request on Sept. 2. Trump’s new executive order, she wrote, “is almost certainly unconstitutional as applied to the certified class for the simple reason that the Supreme Court in Barbara already decided that the children in the class are citizens at birth.” She temporarily prohibited the Trump administration from enforcing the new executive order against any member of the class that she had certified in the case – that is, children who would have been denied citizenship by the 2025 order.

The government asked Boardman to revoke her ruling or pause it. It argued that the named plaintiffs (and, by extension, the rest of the class) do not have a legal right to sue, known as standing, to challenge the 2026 order because their children were born before that order was issued. “Every relevant agency tasked with implementing the 2026 Executive Order,” it wrote, “has now issued public guidance and regulations explicitly saying that ‘the EO is prospective only,’ that the ‘guidance’ therefore too ‘is prospective only,’ and that the guidance ‘will become effective upon publication subject to any legal obligations,’ such as this ongoing preliminary injunction.” And in any event, the government continued, the “[p]laintiffs’ fears of being affected by the 2026 Executive Order” are “based on rife speculation.”

The American Civil Liberties Union, which also represents the plaintiffs in Barbara, filed another case, Nathan v. Trump, in Washington, D.C., in September specifically to challenge the second executive order. The plaintiffs in Nathan include an Australian citizen who, with his wife, is expecting a child through surrogacy who was scheduled to be born in the United States in October and a citizen of a Western European company who lives in the United States and is expecting a baby in April 2027; both she and her husband work for international organizations. They sought to represent a class of children and parents of children born in the United States who would be subject to the second executive order.

The case was assigned to U.S. District Judge Christopher Cooper, who has scheduled a hearing for Thursday on the challengers’ motion.

As for the role of Congress, the court’s decision in Barbara reaffirmed a broad conception of birthright citizenship under the Constitution, potentially leaving relatively little for a Democratic Congress to do – even if it could override a presidential veto.

In February, the Supreme Court struck down the extensive tariffs that President Donald Trump imposed in a series of executive orders in 2025. By a vote of 6-3, the justices ruled that the tariffs exceeded the powers given to the president by Congress under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act, known as IEEPA, a 1977 law that authorizes the president to regulate commerce during national emergencies created by foreign threats.

In its ruling in Learning Resources v. Trump, the court did not address whether or how the federal government should provide refunds to the importers who have paid the tariffs, estimated in 2025 at more than $200 billion. Although the government has now paid out more than $100 billion in refunds, that omission has (perhaps unsurprisingly) also led to litigation.

In V.O.S. Selections v. Trump, one of the original challenges to the IEEPA tariffs, the government is challenging the decision by Judge Richard Eaton of the U.S. Court of International Trade to enter an order that (among other things) requires the government to refund tariffs paid by importers that had not filed their own lawsuits and whose imports had been liquidated – that is, the customs duty had been finally settled – more than 80 days ago. The government contends that such an order amounts to an impermissible universal injunction, under which his order would apply broadly. The government contends that an importer can only obtain a refund if it has a court order allowing it to do so.

On Oct. 8, Eaton certified a class of importers who had paid the IEEPA tariffs but had not yet filed their lawsuits seeking a refund; if the lawsuit goes forward, it would presumably eliminate the need for each importer to file its own lawsuit to obtain a refund.

There are also several pending lawsuits involving the Trump administration’s efforts to impose new tariffs in the wake of the Supreme Court’s ruling on the IEEPA tariffs. On Feb. 20, Trump imposed sweeping tariffs under Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974, which gives the president the power to impose temporary (up to 150 days) surcharges when there are serious international payment problems. Trump relied on his finding of such problems that “significantly harm United States national interests,” citing as an example “a substantial trade deficit.”

In May, the CIT ruled – by a vote of 2-1 – in favor of the states and companies challenging the Section 122 tariffs. The majority concluded that the grounds on which Trump’s proclamation imposing the tariffs relied did not fall within the meaning of the statute.

The government appealed the CIT’s ruling to the Federal Circuit, which paused that decision while the appeal moves forward – allowing the government to continue to collect the tariffs until July 24, when they expired.

With the Section 122 tariffs slated to expire, Trump on July 23 imposed tariffs on goods from 60 U.S. trading partners, including Canada, the United Kingdom, Japan, Australia, Vietnam, Mexico, and South Korea. In doing so, he relied on Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974, which gives the president the power to impose tariffs based on a determination that a country has failed either “to prohibit or effectively enforce” a ban on the imported goods produced with forced labor and that failure has a negative effect on U.S. trade.

In early August, a group of 25 states, led by Oregon, challenged the Section 301 tariffs in the CIT. They contended that the Trump administration was using Section 301 and concerns about forced labor “as a pretext to continue its illegal tariff scheme.” In particular, they alleged, “there is no rational fit between the purported problem of forced labor in international supply chains and the blanket global tariffs the USTR imposed.” Learning Resources, which was one of the challengers in the IEEPA case, filed a similar case; the CIT heard oral arguments in that case, which was designated as a representative case, on Sept. 30.

Here too the newly Democratic Congress – which under the Constitution has the power to impose tariffs but has delegated the authority to control tariff policies to the president – could amend the tariff laws to narrow or strip Trump’s power, subject (as noted above) to a presidential veto.

Amy Howe is the co-founder of SCOTUSblog and its primary reporter.

Family Meal Planning on a Budget – Let the Week’s Sales Set the Menu

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Before you shop, spend 20 minutes planning the week’s dinners from what’s on sale and what’s already in your kitchen. Pick five dinners that share ingredients, save a night for leftovers, keep a backup meal on hand, and write your grocery list from that plan. You’ll make fewer trips and order less takeout.

Without a plan, you pick dinner in the store, where whatever looks good goes in the cart. Then you pick it again at 5:30 on a busy night, when takeout is easier than turning those groceries into a meal.

A plan saves money because it settles what you’ll buy before you walk in. Start it from the week’s sales, and the cheapest meat and produce set the menu. Start it from the fridge and pantry, and you eat what you already paid for before it spoils.

Say your family orders takeout twice a week at about $40 an order. This week chicken thighs and ground beef are on sale, so the plan includes tacos, sheet-pan chicken, and a double pot of chili, with Thursday for the leftovers. If the leftovers night and the backup meal replace both orders, and the extra groceries for them cost about $20, you save about $60 a week.

To make it stick, keep a two-week rotation of 10 dinners your family already likes, and let the kids pick one night’s dinner from it. A meal planning app can save that rotation and turn each week’s dinners into a grocery list.

Put the 20 minutes on the calendar for the evening before your shopping day, and write nothing on the list until the five dinners are set.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang Resigns After Affair with Suspected Chinese Spy—Inside His $53 Million Property Portfolio

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has resigned after having extramarital affairs with a business executive—Alice Huang, who the U.S. government has determined to be working with Chinese intelligence—and a Nvidia employee.

Finance chief Colette Kress will work as the interim CEO while the company looks for a permanent replacement, Nvidia said Thursday.

External and internal candidates will be considered as the company searches for a Jensen's successor.

“Jensen’s contributions to Nvidia are profound. Nvidia is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world," the statement read. "The board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry."

The news follows a Nov. 8 statement from Huang, who admitted to the affairs.

“I was unfaithful to my wife,” Huang stated in a previous message to the company. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

While the board of Nvidia claimed it had “no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised,” an external counsel will be selected to investigate

The revelation, first reported by the New York Times, is expected to have an economic impact not only on Nvidia—whose stock price has already taken a hit—but on the artificial intelligence industry as a whole, given the chipmaker’s influence.

The company was the first in the world to reach a valuation of $5 trillion after seeing unprecedented growth amid the artificial intelligence bom.

According to the New York Times, Nvidia is responsible for making "the most essential and expensive component in almost every A.I. scheme," a collection of hardware designed specifically to bolster artificial intelligence.

Its ascent to the pinnacle of tech success began as something of a slow burn in 1993, when Huang, 63, launched the company alongside Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem.

At the time, Nvidia was viewed very much as a niche—and fledgling—tech startup. However, it has since grown into an industry giant, inking deals with all manner of brands and agencies, from the U.S. Department of Energy to Nokia, Samsung, and Sam Altman's company, OpenAI, the parent to ChatGPT.

Huang's own net worth has skyrocketed with that accelerated growth. He is currently one of the 10 richest men in the world with an estimated fortune of $174 billion, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

The number of men who can claim to top him in personal fortune can be counted on two hands—and include the likes of Elon Musk, Jeff Bezos, Mark Zuckerberg, and Larry Ellison.

Like others in the exclusive "super billionaires" club—men and women with net worths exceeding $50 billion—Huang has invested his fortune across diverse ventures and companies, while managing to remain in this elite group.

However, he has also followed in the footsteps of his fellow tech titans by pouring a sizable chunk of his fortune into real estate—amassing an incredibly impressive portfolio of homes from Hawaii to California.

Huang, who was born in Taiwan in 1963, spent his early childhood in Thailand. He moved to the U.S. after his father visited America and vowed to bring his sons, he previously told CNBC.

Ahead of his move, his mom would teach him 10 words from the dictionary each day to help him learn English, despite not knowing the language herself.

At age 9, Huang and his brother headed to America to live with their relatives. His early life in the States was starkly different from the current lavish life he lives.

When he was a child, his aunt and uncle accidentally enrolled him in a Baptist boarding school in Kentucky, thinking it was a prestigious prep school.

While there, he became a target for bullies and teasing due to his long hair and accent.

As the future of Nvidia's stock remains unclear, Realtor.com® has lifted the lid on the tech tycoon's expansive property portfolio.

In 2017, the tech tycoon splashed over $38 million for a sprawling mansion in San Francisco's Gold Coast neighborhood.

Property records seen by Realtor.com reveal that the dwelling is tied to Riva LLC, which is managed by Michael A. Chojnacki, a tax lawyer who looks after the finances of Huang and his wife, Lori.

The luxe abode in the Pacific Heights neighborhood spans 11,400 square feet and boasts a curved roof, panoramic views of the Golden Gate Bridge, and many bedrooms and bathrooms.

The limestone mansion has a large terrace, a three-car garage, two wine rooms, an outdoor kitchen, an elevator, a theater, and a gym.

Developer Bill Campbell, of Marble Management, previously told the Wall Street Journal that construction on the dwelling took nearly four years.

Of course, Huang is also neighbors with Ellison and David Sachs.

Huang previously owned two starter homes in San Jose, CA. The first one, which measured 1,500 square feet and had three bedrooms, was sold for $185,000 in 1988—just five years before he founded Nvidia.

Elsewhere in California, the tech mogul owns a lavish dwelling in Los Altos, just 15 miles west of Nvidia's headquarters, which is in Santa Clara County.

He purchased the property in 2003 for $6.9 million. It measures over 7,000 square feet and has six bedrooms.

The elegant two-story estate features lush landscaping and a resort-style pool.

To top off his property portfolio, the businessman also snapped up a home on the south side of Maui in 2004.

The dwelling is situated in the Wailea Resort, which houses 14 other properties behind a gate and spans 10.5 acres on the ocean.

The home features seven bedrooms and was built in 2008. He paid $7.8 million for the abode.

The property spans 8,000 square feet and is surrounded by lush greenery.

In 2023, Maui experienced deadly wildfires, and the Huangs "donated significantly to local relief efforts,” an Nvidia spokesperson said at the time.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Aura Entertainment has acquired U.S. rights to Flint, the Western written and directed by Ryan Whitaker (Surprised by Oxford) and based on the novel by Louis L’Amour.

The film stars Josh Holloway (Yellowstone) alongside an ensemble cast that includes Sarah Gadon, Max Martini, Dianna Agron, Mike Vogel, Charles Esten, Forrie J. Smith, Bailey Chase, Nick Searcy, and Kristoffer Polaha.

The movie world premiered at the 57th Annual Nashville Film Festival in September. Aura us due to release in theaters in spring 2027. Related Stories News Dylan Sprouse, Barbie Ferreira & Tanner Buchanan Lead Hamptons Thriller 'The Sleeping Dogs Of Southampton' -- AFM News Pierson Fodé & Matteo Lane Join Lucy Hale In Fantasy Rom-Com 'Vision Board' -- AFM

Flint “follows James Kettleman (Holloway), a ruthless East Coast businessman who returns to the Montana frontier he once fled and takes the name of the notorious gunfighter who raised him. When he is drawn into a violent range war alongside a determined female rancher, he has to reckon with his past and decide what kind of legacy he will leave behind.” Watch on Deadline

The deal was negotiated by Marc Goldberg on behalf of Aura and UTA Independent Film Group and attorney Sam Curphey on behalf of the filmmakers. Capture is handling international sales.

The film was produced by Ken Carpenter of Tirian Films, with Jerilyn Esquibel, Josh Holloway, Beau L’Amour, and Ari Novak also producing. Beau L’Amour is the son of Louis L’Amour. Mark Pentecost executive-produced under his production banner, The Pentecost Group.

“Flint is a Western in the classic tradition, made for the big screen, and Josh Holloway gives it real grit and weight,” said Aura Entertainment’s Marc Goldberg. “Louis L’Amour has generations of readers who’ve waited a long time to see this story in theaters, and we’re proud to be the ones bringing it to them.”

“It was an incredible honor to adapt this legendary novel. I set out to make a film in the spirit of the Western and noir pictures I love—with life-or-death stakes, visceral action, and emotional impact. I’m thrilled to be partnering with Aura to bring it to audiences in the format I always envisioned: on the big screen,” added writer-director Ryan Whitaker.

Jussi Jääskeläinen praises Arsenal star David Raya

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Former Premier League goalkeeper Jussi Jääskeläinen thinks David Raya is the best goalkeeper in the division right now.

But the former Bolton Wanderers star feels the Arsenal No.1 has more to prove before he can be put up with the all-time greats of the competition.

Jääskeläinen recently spoke to Kasinopartio.com, where he praised Raya’s performances and the understanding he has developed with Arsenal’s defenders.

“Raya has been a fantastic signing. He has a great relationship with the centre-backs; even when the back four changes, everything works well.”

The Spaniard has played a key role for Mikel Arteta since his arrival from Brentford. His organisation of the backline, key saves and contribution to Arsenal’s build up play have helped him to be a standout player.

In 2012, Raya began his journey in English football when he moved to Blackburn Rovers from Spain. At that time, he was just a young teenager unfamiliar with English football.

His short spell at Southport introduced him to senior football. Soon, he returned to Blackburn and forged a career ahead of moving to Brentford in 2019. After four years with Brentford, Raya achieved success at the club, helping them to the Premier League.

The player’s performances in the higher leagues caught the attention of Arsenal, and Arteta signed him on loan to the Emirates in 2023 before making the move permanent the following summer.

It didn’t take long for Raya to establish himself in the team. He took home the Premier League Golden Glove, having achieved 16 clean sheets in 32 appearances.

This was a significant milestone in his first season with the Arsenal team, and he has since remained as number one, playing a key role in the club’s pursuit of titles.

While Jääskeläinen said Raya is the best goalkeeper in the Premier League at the moment, he does not think he should be up there with the most experienced.

He believes other goalkeepers have earned an advantage through their consistency over a longer period.

“You have lots of top goalkeepers that I think are still a little bit ahead because of the years they’ve done it in the Premier League.”

“He’s not up there with them yet, but he’s definitely going in the right direction.”

Raya has already proven he can play to the top standard. However, it will be his consistency moving forward that will define his long-term reputation.

Petr Čech, Peter Schmeichel and Edwin van der Sar have all gained great reputations over the years. There is still plenty of time for Raya to do the same, but he will need to keep up his high standards whilst wearing an Arsenal shirt.

At just 31, Raya has a lot left in his career. If he can continue playing at this level, it will make the debate around whether he is one of the best goalkeepers in the Premier League even more intriguing.

Andorra goalkeeper Jesus Owono undecided on football return after Jesus de Miguel’s tragic death

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Jesus de Miguel died last week following an on-pitch collision with Andorra goalkeeper Jesus Owono

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

FC Andorra Carles Manso said on Thursday that goalkeeper Jesus Owono has not yet decided whether he will return to competition following the death of Tenerife forward Jesus de Miguel.

De Miguel died in hospital on 4 November, the day after a collision with Owono on the pitch during a Spanish football match.

Owono, who is Equatorial Guinea's goalkeeper, released a statement through Andorra last week in which he described the ordeal as "the most difficult days of my life."

Manso has now offered an update on Jesus Owono, telling a press conference: "What we have to do is support him in every way and be close to him.

"We will give him space so that he feels completely free to make any decision, whether he wants to return, when, or if he does not want to return."

Manso said Owono was spending time with his family, friends and partner and described the aftermath of last week’s tragedy as a "very complicated situation to manage”.

"When he feels it is appropriate and when he feels genuinely ready to take a decision like that (he will do so)," added Manso.

Owono has 47 caps for Equatorial Guinea. He was not called up for his country's doubleheader with Zimbabwe during this international break.

Andorra travel to Almeria on Saturday for their first fixture since De Miguel's death.

Over the weekend, the Tenerife players and thousands of fans paid an emotional final tribute to De Miguel at the club’s stadium.

A massive banner displaying his black-and-white photograph and the words "Forever Demi", a reference to the player’s nickname, hung behind a central pitch stage at the Estadio Heliodoro Rodriguez Lopez.

Following a video showing footage of his Tenerife debut, fans chanted: "De Miguel, we love you".

Players sat beside representatives from all clubs in Spain's top two divisions, including Real Madrid director Emilio Butragueno, as tears fell in the stands.

"To speak of you in the past tense is tremendously unfair," Tenerife captain Enric Gallego said, pausing repeatedly during his speech.

"It's still hard to believe that this has happened to us, Demi; you'll always be one of us."

"We're still struggling to come to terms with the fact that you won't be with us again.”

Tarantino gets candid on table read issues in a new book

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Quentin Tarantino revealed he once fired one of his Inglourious Basterds lead actors after a "terrible" table read.

In an excerpt from Jay Glennie's The Making of Quentin Tarantino’s Inglourious Basterds (via People), the director said Mélanie Laurent – who played cinema owner Shosanna in the 2009 film – didn't make a good first impression.

"Mélanie Laurent’s table read was terrible," Tarantino recalled, adding, "it wasn’t good enough to be terrible."

Tarantino wrote of the experience, "A table read with the whole cast is so exciting and so important to me. Well, Mélanie was terrible, so I fired her."

Of course, that wasn't the end for the French actor. Laurent said she was ill and would wait to read again for Tarantino – who agreed to "turn the clock" back across multiple days of rehearsals.

Some on Inglourious Basterds weren't so lucky, however. Tarantino said Isabelle Huppert was replaced by Maggie Cheung for a role that was ultimately cut after missing the table read entirely.

On chopping and changing actors prior to filming, Tarantino remarked, “I have fired actors in the past. If it’s not right, it is not right. I don’t owe them shit. I owe my film the best actors I can find. Period.”

Inglourious Basterds stands as one of Tarantino's most iconic, and instantly quotable, movies. Starring Brad Pitt, Christoph Waltz, Eli Roth, and Michael Fassbender, follows an alt-history revolution against Nazi leadership in 1940s France.

Next up from Tarantino is new play The Popinjay Cavalier, starring Sacha Baron Cohen. The production is set to debut in London's West End from February 2027. The wait, then, for the director's 10th – and possibly final – film goes on.

Bitcoin Price Crashes to $72,700 as Leveraged Traders Buy the Dip: Is the Bottom In?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The crypto market crash deepens as $1B+ in longs get liquidated. Bitcoin sits mid-range at $72,969; fear at 28 isn’t a bottom yet.

Bitcoin fell below $73,000 on Thursday, November 12, extending a week-long crypto market crash in which traders who bet on a rebound were forced out of their positions again and again.

Bitcoin touched $72,735 on Binance, a hair above Tuesday’s $72,727 low and its weakest price since August 20. It trades at $72,969, down 9.4% from the November 4 close.

BREAKING: $BTC just fell to its lowest level since August.

Over $210 million worth of longs liquidated in the past 24 hours.

The dumping continues… pic.twitter.com/R7NVGivFnD

— Crypto Rover (@cryptorover) November 12, 2026

The altcoins are doing worse. Over seven days, Ethereum has lost 16.4%, Solana 14.7% and XRP 21.3%. CoinGecko values the whole crypto market at $2.42 trillion on Thursday, and Bitcoin makes up 60.5% of it.

Leverage made every drop bigger. More than $1 billion of bets were liquidated on Monday alone, almost all of them longs, the positions that profit only if prices rise. Liquidation means the exchange closes a trade by force once the trader’s collateral runs out, and those forced sales push the price down further.

So is this the flush that ends the crypto market slide, or is there more to come? The data has an answer, and it is not a comforting one.

The sell-off had no single cause. Four pieces of bad news landed one after another:

Each hit forced out more leveraged longs across the crypto market. Thursday’s slide continued the pattern on a smaller scale. We added up every forced closure in Bitcoin, Ether, Solana, XRP and Dogecoin futures on OKX, one of the largest exchanges, over the past 24 hours. Longs lost $27.6 million, against $12.3 million for shorts.

EXPLORE: Bitcoin Price Slides Toward $73K as Nvidia Plunges 9% and MSCI Drops Saylor’s Strategy

Big liquidations usually clear out the crowd. This time, the crowd came back.

In plain terms, traders keep buying every dip with borrowed money. That leaves fuel for another round of forced selling if Bitcoin drops through the next support. Bottoms tend to come when funding turns negative and open interest falls hard, a sign that leveraged buyers have given up.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Next Crypto to Hit 1 Dollar

The Crypto Fear & Greed Index, which scores market mood from 0 (extreme fear) to 100 (extreme greed), reads 28 today. Fear is usually read as a buy signal. The history says it is not enough on its own.

We checked every day since February 2018 when the index sat at 28 or lower, 943 days in all, and where Bitcoin stood later.

This year’s two bottoms came with real panic. When Bitcoin hit $60,000 on February 6, the index read 9. When it bottomed at $57,800 on July 1, it read 11. Today’s 28 is fear, but it is not capitulation, the moment sellers give up all at once.

On the weekly chart, where each candle covers one week, Bitcoin has spent 2026 in a wide range. The low is $57,800 from July 1 and the high is $87,396 from late September. The midpoint is $72,598, almost exactly where Bitcoin trades now.

The price lost its 50-week exponential moving average (EMA), an average that gives recent prices more weight, at $78,219. Its 200-week EMA sits at $69,597. Bitcoin closed below that line for 16 weeks this year, in February and March and again from June to mid-August, and both of the year’s bottoms formed during those stretches.

For the crypto market, three signs would say the bottom is close: the fear index dropping toward the teens, funding turning negative, and open interest falling as longs give up. None has shown up yet. Until they do, the 200-week EMA at $69,597 is the line that separates a pullback inside the 2026 range from a retest of the summer lows.

Jensen Huang has resigned from his role as CEO and president of Nvidia. He will be replaced by interim CEO, Colette Kress, who had previously worked as the company's CFO for a long stretch.

"Nvidia Board of Directors announced today that Jensen Huang has resigned as President, Chief Executive Officer, and a member of the Board, effective immediately," a statement on Nvidia's newsroom says.

Jensen Huang has been embroiled in a scandal, or rather a couple of scandals, over the past few weeks.

First, an affair to which he later admitted; suggested to have been with a datacentre company executive suspected of working for Chinese intelligence.

Second, another alleged affair, this time with an executive at Nvidia.

The markets may get spooked by the announcement, as Huang was a co-founder of the business (founded in 1993, making Huang the longest CEO of a major tech firm) and has overseen the company through its latest and largest success.

The share price for the company had already plummeted to its lowest levels since June 2025 following a report that the company's board was considering ousting Huang.

The PC Gamer Circuit Breaker newsletter breaks down the latest PC gaming tech, and finds you the best deals on the best products

The company's share price is currently down around 12% in pre-market trading.

The move to appoint the company's long-standing CFO as interim CEO was an expected one, to help ease the transition from Huang's leadership. Intel did something similar when the company ousted its CEO in 2018.

"Colette Kress, current Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of Nvidia, has agreed to serve as Interim Chief Executive Officer. Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named. The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates."

Huang has also resigned from his position as president and from his board seat, which may ease some concerns in recent days that his significant stake and power in the company would be tough to expunge should the company board try to push him out.

Now, the job for the remaining Nvidia employees is to find the next full-time CEO. That could take some time.

Viktor Gyokeres reveals Arsenal transfer stance amid performance criticism

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Viktor Gyokeres has insisted he wants to stay at Arsenal, as he dismissed criticism of his performances.

The striker was the club’s top scorer in his first season at the Emirates Stadium and played his part in the Gunners ending their 22-year wait for a Premier League title.

However, he has looked an uncomfortable fit for this Arsenal side for large periods and Mikel Arteta has consistently favoured Kai Havertz over the Swede in the biggest matches.

The No14 has started only one match in the league for Arsenal this season, missing out on a place in the side even after scoring and assisting against Slavia Prague in the Champions League.

Despite that frustration, Gyokeres was clear when asked by Fotbollskanalen whether he wanted to stay at Arsenal.

“It feels good when I’ve played. We’ve won most of the games. It was a tough loss in the cup [Fleetwood]. Otherwise it’s felt good for myself and the team. I’ve been doing well lately when I’ve been playing. Right now it feels good.

“The communication with Arteta is good. We talk about football and how we should play. Before a match, if I’m going to start, we talk about the opponents and what I should think about.”

Gyokeres has faced criticism for his performances, particularly early in the season when he had a run of looking well off the pace.

Despite those displays improving, Troy Deeney has been among those to still take aim at the striker, claiming the Arsenal players do not trust Gyokeres to give him the ball on the pitch.

“Sometimes it might get to me, but overall not so much,” Gyokeres said in response to the critics.

“If I score goals and assists in the Champions League, I couldn’t care less what they say.

“It’s nice to do those kinds of performances. To deliver for myself and for the team. That’s where the focus lies.

“It’s natural too. It’s what gets the most headlines and what sticks the most when people listen. I understand that’s how it is.”

Guangyun Chen promises an "optimised client" to reduce stuttering and performance issues.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

"Recently, there's been some debate in the community regarding the use of mods and configs. Our stance is clear: Marvel Rivals is a fair competitive game. Any use of mods or configs to get an unfair combat advantage is unacceptable, and we will take action against it. This is our bottom line.

"These forbidden modifications include altering maps into minimalist 'potato' models, amplifying footstep audio, removing screen shake, and so on. Furthermore, some of these modifications employ memory injection. Since this behaviour is functionally identical to using cheats, we will handle them accordingly." via a dev diary.

How far would you go to achieve decent frame rates? Well some Marvel Rivals players have been breaking the rules to try and give themselves a better shot of having a sort of salvageable in-game experience.

Sure, a lot of players using mods and configs are doing so to give themselves the "unfair combat advantage" that Guangyun Chen talks about, using it to dial down the map detail so enemy players stand out more or heightening environmental tells like footsteps. But that doesn't account for absolutely everyone within the community, with some changing their configs to try and remove the stuttering that can plague Marvel Rivals games.

Marvel Rivals has always had an issue with its performance. I shrugged it off as teething pain at first but it's been almost two years since release, and not much has changed. Five months ago NetEase released an "experimental feature" to try and dial down the game's insatiable appetite for RAM, and while it reduced the frequency of game crashes, it didn't solve stuttering.

Then the decision was then made in July to enact some changes that could help with the visual clutter players experience during games. It was brought on by the addition of Jubilee, who had particularly messy VFX. I didn't notice much of a change; team fights are still as chaotic, messy, and confusing as ever.

The biggest change for good happened a few months ago when Marvel Rivals managed to shave an impressive 40GB off by completely repackaging its assets. But by and large things haven't improved fast enough, and Guangyun Chen even accepts that players messing with configs for better performance do have a valid point.

"We also recognise that a core desire behind players using mods or tweaking configs is simply wanting better framerates and a cleaner visual environment," Guangyun Chen adds. "We are actively working on this. In season 11, thanks to the hard work of our programming team, we'll be rolling out an optional, experimental optimised client.

"This optimisation targets the logic load on the CPU during matches. When toggled on, it will provide up to a 10% boost in CPU performance and significantly reduce mid-combat stuttering. You can download the corresponding DLC on PC and then enable this experimental feature directly in the game launcher."

I am, however, not yet convinced. I've been waiting for huge improvements in Marvel Rivals for so long now that I'll only believe it when I see it.

The Witcher 3 uses gen-AI to enhance Geralt's Polish voice following actor's stroke - "I do think this is one of those ways where you can use AI ethically"

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

"I have great trust ... for the creators of this game."

CD Projekt Red has revealed that it used - and is using - generative AI technology to treat and alter the voice of the Polish actor who portrayed Geralt of Rivia in upcoming Witcher 3 expansion Songs of the Past.

The actor, Jacek Rozenek, has portrayed Geralt of Rivia in all the major Witcher games, meaning his voice has become as synonymous with the role in Poland as Doug Cockle's has for people playing in English. But Rozenek suffered a severe stroke in 2019 that left him temporarily unable to speak, and when he was able to talk and perform again, the tonality and quality of his voice had changed.

It's for these reasons that CD Projekt Red - with Rozenek's consent - employed the help of a company called Respeecher to alter the lines he recorded for The Witcher 3's Songs of the Past expansion using generative-AI technology. As confirmed to Game File, an AI trained on Rozenek's previous Witcher work is retreating his recordings to make them sound more like the Geralt you hear elsewhere in The Witcher 3.

"The point is that this voice should be as close as possible to my voice from 12 years ago," Jacek Rozenek explained. "I have great trust ... for the creators of this game."

Game File heard both treated and untreated recordings of Rozenek's Songs of the Past work, and reported a "significant" difference between them. Rozenek's untreated voice was apparently "deep and gravelly" and "noticeably rougher than his recordings for the original game". But treated, the voice was "far smoother and sounds younger", and closer to the original Witcher 3 Geralt recordings.

This isn't the first time CD Projekt Red has used generative AI for voice recordings in one of its games. The studio worked with Respeecher in 2023 to produce new voiceover lines for Cyberpunk 2077 expansion Phantom Liberty. Viktor Vektor actor Miłogost "Miłek" Reczek passed away in 2021, after the original game was released, and CD Projekt Red didn't want to recast the role. Reczek's sons apparently gave their blessing for their father's voice to be artificially generated.

"I do think this is one of those ways where you can use AI ethically with consent and also to do something good," CD Projekt Red localisation producer Mikołaj Szwed told Game File, referring to the Rozenek recordings.

"For future projects, when we consider Geralt is becoming older and has a deeper voice, this is not a problem for us at all. It's more about this specific situation here, where we have both the voice from 12 years ago and from now next to each other, which could kind of take people out of the experience and feel that there is this difference."

The third Witcher 3 expansion, Songs of the Past, releases at some point next year, an unprecedented 12 years after the original game launched. It adds an entirely new region, Letten, and centres its main story around Geralt's missing bardic bestie Dandelion, whose family lives there.

In preparation for the expansion, a remastered version of The Witcher 3 was released a couple of months ago, overhauling the old role-playing adventure and bringing its baseline presentation more in line with what we'd expect from a modern game; it also overhauled some animations and enemy behaviours and reworked skill trees. For that reason, old-gen consoles PS4 and Xbox One won't receive the Songs of the Past expansion, despite The Witcher 3 being available on them.

What's The Worst Way You've Abused A Rental Car?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Unless you only travel within driving distance of home or are willing to spend the time and money on shipping, we will all end up in a situation where renting a car is a necessity. There may be plenty of cities with fantastic public transportation systems, but you just need a car to venture out and explore the countryside. Many of us are far more care-free behind the wheel when the vehicle is owned by an abstract corporate entity and not a person with a face.

What's the worst way you've abused a rental car? I'm not talking about returning it with an empty tank. That's just a surcharge that rental companies are more than happy to add to your bill. Abuse is real, lasting damage that's an absolute nightmare to reverse. Let me use a hypothetical beach day in the South of France as an example. You and a few friends take a decent Peugeot 408 to a sleepy village on the Mediterranean. After a fun day of sand and surf, everyone piles back into the sedan right off the beach, covering the interior with sand. That's the appetizer. On the drive back, you get impatient sitting behind a truck on a two-lane road. You pull into the oncoming lane but don't get entirely clear, and you sideswipe the truck. The 408 is far worse off, with its entire right side scratched up. That's abuse!

Besides outright crashing a rental car, the worst thing you could do is race a pack of them. Rental car racing actually is a significant part of NASCAR lore. The mischievous tradition has endured stock car racing's evolution from a moonshiners' pastime to a billion-dollar sport. Several current Cup Series drivers have admitted to racing each other and their team members. In many instances, rental car racing is just a fun way to salvage a rained-out test day. Everyone just piles on to the track once the showers stop and run laps to dry the racing surface. On race day, it's pushing the limit on the drive out of the track. Joe Gibbs Racing driver Chase Briscoe told The Athletic in 2025, "Honestly, the craziest thing, I think, is watching the team guys leave a racetrack. It is like the best racing and just the most chaos you've ever seen in your life."

Remember to leave your stories of rental car abuse in the comments section below. Be sure to mention the make or model of the car that you returned to be someone else's problem.

After two weeks of multiplayer downtime, Dark Souls 2 & 3's mysterious server "issue" has been fixed, so it no longer feels so lonely online

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Developer FromSoftware has announced that online multiplayer servers for Dark Souls 2 and 3 are back online following a nearly two-week outage.

FromSoftware temporarily disabled multiplayer on 30th October, citing what it called "a server issue". Some speculated this could be related to the recently discovered PHP security bug, which impacted a number of other games.

FromSoftware never specified the exact issue impacting its servers, and today's statement is equally vague. "Regarding the temporary suspension of the game server for Dark Souls 2, and Dark Souls 3, the investigation has been completed and the fix has been applied. The game server and online features are now restored," a notice on Dark Souls' official site reads.

"We sincerely apologise for the inconvenience caused, and thank you for your patience and understanding."

Online servers do more in FromSoftware games than simply facilitating co-op and PvP; they're also responsible for handling messages players leave for each other in-game, as well as the ghostly representations of players who just went through the same area, helping the Dark Souls series' forlorn worlds feel a little less solitary.

This isn't the first time Dark Souls servers have gone offline, either. In 2022, servers for all three games went offline due to a vulnerability that allowed malicious code to run on them. In that instance, it took FromSoftware three months to bring servers back online for some games, and as many as nine months for others.

In other FromSoftware news, the studio recently announced - following months of speculation - it's officially delaying the Switch 2-exclusive The Duskbloods to sometime in 2027.

Cairo Film Festival Artistic Director Mohamed Tarek on Trusting Audiences: They ‘Are Often More Adventurous Than We Imagine’

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

For Mohamed Tarek, one of the biggest lessons from his first year as artistic director of the Cairo Intl. Film Festival came from an unexpected source – the audience. After drawing approximately 45,000 ticket sales last year, the festival is building on that response with an expanded program that challenges traditional screening hierarchies, introduces new initiatives for younger audiences and brings films to more venues across the Egyptian capital.

“The most important lesson was that audiences are often more adventurous than we imagine,” Tarek tells Variety as CIFF celebrates 50 years since its founding. The festival’s 47th edition runs Nov. 11–20. Related Stories Charli xcx Joins Martin Scorsese's 'What Happens at Night' With Leonardo DiCaprio and Jennifer Lawrence (EXCLUSIVE)

One particularly revealing experience for Tarek was the enthusiastic reception for short films screened in the Cairo Opera House’s main theater, a venue traditionally associated with major premieres and feature competition titles.

“It challenged the assumption that certain kinds of cinema necessarily belong in smaller venues or are intended only for specialized audiences,” he explains.

That response has encouraged Tarek to give restored classics, short films and special screenings greater prominence this year, including at the festival’s major venues.

“What changed, fundamentally, was our confidence in the audience,” he says. “Instead of anticipating their limitations, we are trying to create more opportunities for discovery, surprise and engagement.”

The 2026 lineup comprises 145 films from 64 countries, selected from approximately 5,600 feature and short-film submissions. “The challenge with 5,600 submissions is to move beyond selecting individual films we admire and create a program in which different works can coexist, complement one another and sometimes challenge one another,” he explains.

The selection includes 17 world premieres and 87 Middle East and North Africa premieres. But for Tarek, securing premieres is only one consideration when selecting films. “I would always choose an exceptional film that has already travelled internationally over a weaker film whose main attraction is its premiere status,” he insists.

Elsewhere, new programming initiatives include Cairo Kids, a children’s film program curated by film critic and programmer Rasha Hosny. Featuring short and feature-length films, both animation and live-action, the program is designed for different age groups.

“We wanted to move beyond the assumption that children’s cinema is necessarily animation, or that all young viewers have the same interests and abilities,” Tarek notes.

CIFF is also doubling the size of its New Wave program that brings aspiring film professionals from across Egypt into the festival ecosystem. Following an inaugural edition with 50 participants, this year’s iteration has selected 100 from more than 850 applications. Seventy come from outside Cairo, representing 23 of the country’s 27 governorates, while women account for 55% of participants.

The program combines screenings, workshops, masterclasses and mentoring, while transportation and accommodation for participants traveling from outside the capital are underwritten by the festival. Its scope extends beyond filmmaking to criticism, programming and distribution.

“If New Wave helps talented young people imagine a future in cinema and gives them some of the tools and connections to pursue it, that would be a meaningful achievement,” Tarek adds.

The festival is also expanding its immersive XR offerings, while its Midnight Screenings and Midnight Classics sections pair contemporary films with older ones, exploring connections across generations of filmmaking.

“A restored classic, a short film, or an immersive experience can be just as significant to a viewer as a competition title,” Tarek argues. “Each deserves thoughtful programming and presentation.”

CIFF is also extending its reach beyond the Cairo Opera House, adding screenings at Zawya, an independent arthouse cinema, and Tahrir Cinema, a popular downtown venue.

Beyond the festival’s programming, Tarek sees encouraging developments in Arab cinema, particularly among filmmakers increasingly confident in telling personal stories without feeling obliged to represent an entire country or society. He cites three Egyptian films in this year’s lineup – Ahmad Abdalla’s “BerleeN_,” Ashgan El-Hamus’ “Al Baraneya” and Sara Shazli’s documentary “Your Daughter.”

Despite that creative momentum, Tarek acknowledges that financing, development and distribution remain major challenges for Arab filmmakers, particularly when it comes to reaching audiences in the region. “A film may travel successfully through international festivals and still struggle to reach audiences in its own country,” he observes.

As CIFF marks a half-century since its first edition, Tarek hopes to expand the festival’s activities beyond its annual November dates and eventually bring more of its programming to other Egyptian governorates.

“For me, the festival’s real influence should continue long after its final screening,” he concludes.
